# Changelog

## 1.1.0

### The CAD operations finished; Settings → Units; solvers of your own; a validation page

- **File ▸ Save as custom block, and a chapter of the tour for it.** The open script is saved as a function in a file of the blocks folder (the model's lines are its body, the settings of the view and the imports of the library are left out, the model that is shown is returned, a number dragged with a gizmo is a number in it): the whole model is then one call in any script and an entry of the right-click menu (Parametric shapes ▸ New custom block). Saving again updates the file; a file of another name is never overwritten. The right-click menus are read again when a block is saved or its folder changes, so the entry is there at once (they used to be read once, and a block saved while FielDes was open was not in them). The tour has a new chapter 4, Import and export (a part in from a STEP file, a mesh out, the block saved, its file in the folder, the block in the menu), and the later chapters are numbered one on. Also in the tour: a step that starts a calculation is done when the calculation has finished (the bar above the console is lit while it runs, and the bar at the bottom of the picture while the result is drawn), every row a step asks to select is framed, each result has a step that isolates it (I), the face steps of the static analysis select the face alone, the load changes direction, the flow is in the order of the static analysis, the foam and the cell are edited after the lattice that shows the change, and the lattice, foam and cell texts are written as the menu writes them.
- **A snapping mode (Tab), for exact placement.** With the viewport in front, Tab turns on a grid of the whole space: a faint violet see-through lattice of the whole space, seen out to a viewing distance -- a slice of it from just in front of the place you look at out to that distance, fading steadily with distance from you (the nearer a line, the stronger, thicker and lighter it is: of two lines side by side you can tell which is nearer), as deep as the distance on the card says -- and at least four rows deep when the view is zoomed in so far that a step is wider than that (a share of the way from you to that place, so the picture of it is the same at every zoom and every pan; where its lines would be closer than about 40 pixels only every 2nd, 5th, 10th ... one is drawn and the line at the bottom says which, so the lattice never vanishes when you zoom out; the three axes are in their colours), a violet frame, and a card in the top right corner -- the step of the grid (automatic, or a number, with half and double; the grid has a size of its own: automatic is a round number from the size of the region that is drawn, a tenth of its longest side, the same at every zoom), the angle step with its presets, the viewing distance of the lattice, snapping to the points that are there. Everything that is moved snaps while it is on: a model dragged by an arrow or a square of its gizmo goes to a node, a face or a point that is pulled does too, and a click that puts down a model or a point lands on one; every turn goes to a multiple of the angle step, and the Shift gizmo turns in that same step whether the mode is on or not (it used to be five degrees, which is where the step starts). Alt held moves freely. The work plane view switches the mode off while it is open and back on when it is left. The guided tour has a step for it (chapter 2, Snap to a grid), and the settings of the card are kept.
- **A radius for the point fields, and the influence of a point.** `point(x, y, z, influence=r)` says how far a point reaches, in mm. `distance_to_point(p, radius=None)` and `radial_field(center, axis, radius=None)` are the distance from the sphere (or the tube) of that radius round the point, negative inside it, 0 on it, positive outside -- a sphere's value as a field --, and the influence of a point given to them is the radius; a point that says no influence is the plain distance as it always was, so no script changes. Clicking the icon of a field in the viewport again closes its viewer (it used to be only a click on empty space that did).
- **The tour, after a morning of use.** The card of the step about pulling a pane out stands in the window the pane went into, and points at the button of that window that puts it back (it stood behind that window, and the arrow went to a corner of this one); the rows of the model tree keep their names at the narrowest panel (a column of buttons that no row uses takes no room, and the panel is 260 pixels at least); the Hold Shift step is done when Shift is held; the Sections step frames the gap above a row and not the row; the optimization of chapter 6 is never read back from a solved problem, so it is seen running; the cell of your own is a strut cell (`custom_truss`: add a node and a beam), the foam gets its skin with `surface=True`, the sweep along a line is made with Ctrl+E or the menu and its picture holds the whole line, the mirror step starts with a table without legs and the user makes one, the Apps step shows a real example app through File ▸ Preview as app, and the arrow of a step about a menu comes up to its button from below.
- **The guided tour is in twelve chapters, and six of them build something with the right-click menus.** The one walk is chapters now, each ending with a card that congratulates and asks whether to go on or to explore alone; leaving that way shows where the tour is found again (Help → Guided tour), which goes on **where you stopped** -- the chapter and the step are kept in the settings -- and the **Chapters** button of every card lists them and goes to the one you choose. 1 How FielDes works (the script is the model, a number changed, every shape is a field and the section view that shows it, a field in place of a number, what that means for design, a lattice that follows a field, an analysis as a field); 2 navigation, gizmos and shortcuts (the standard views, your own navigation, panes pulled out into windows, the gizmo); 3 modelling with objects and the model tree (with **snapping one body onto another**); 4 work planes, 2D shapes, paths and extrusions; 5 symmetry; 6 the special features in short (lattices, simulations and optimization, flows, manufacturing, apps); and 7 to 12 **build a lattice, a static analysis, a flow, a topology optimization, a manufacturing optimization and a FielDes app**, step by step: select the models, right-click, choose the entry (Engineering → Structural → static_analysis...), type over a placeholder or a number; **Show me** does it with a pretend hand (Ctrl+click on the rows, the right-click, the entries one after the other, the typing) and puts everything back; a step starts from the model the menus write, so it works whether or not the step before it was done. The place in the tour is a small line above the title of the card, so a long title breaks into lines instead of being cut off. Every text of the tour -- the cards, the chapter menu, the chapter-end cards, the buttons -- is translated into Spanish, French, German, Japanese, Chinese and Russian (166 new texts; the names of the code stay as they are), and a card of each language was looked at in a window of its own. (`app/src/tutorial.cpp`, [docs/interface.md](docs/interface.md#the-guided-tour).)
- **Typing over a placeholder works, in the tour and everywhere.** Found by the tour: a call that was open in the editor because it has a placeholder (`o=...`) was *folded* by the editor the moment the first character was typed over the three dots (the line is no longer a placeholder, so nothing held it open), and the rest of the typing went to the first line of the call, where there is no field. The editor does not fold a call while the text cursor is in its lines, and the tree keeps a row open while its line is being written in (`Editor::setFold`, `ScenePanel::applyShowOnly`); the tour keeps such a call open too. Seen in a plain window and in the step "Placeholders" of the tour.
- **A part whose faces lie on integer coordinates no longer grows small wings at the foot of a wall in the picture.** Found by a question about the manufacturing optimization: the four bottom corners of the stem of the T were drawn with a wing, 0.75 mm sideways and 0.5 mm below the floor at resolution 3 (about 0.4 mm at 5, none at 10), in the window only -- the exported mesh, the numbers and the checks never had it. Measured: the field of the drawn copy differs from the part's by at most 0.00009 mm there, the same field exported as an STL at the same resolution has no vertex outside the stem's box, and with the region of the picture moved by 0.11 mm so that no face lies on a plane of the mesher's grid the window draws none. Traced in the mesher (`FIELDES_DC_TRACE=x,y,z,r` prints what the cells within r of a point were given, and every sample): at such a corner -- three faces tied exactly -- the field gave a **derivative of length 1.1e-8**, the float noise of a branch with no slope, and `Intersection::push` normalises it, so the plane through the foot of that sample lay **8000 mm** away (AtB of -5500 where the others had 12) and the vertex of the cell went to the end of its leash, one cell width out. A sample whose plane lies farther from it than two cells is no longer made a plane (`DCTree::saveIntersection`; it keeps its place in the mass point, as a sample with no derivative did). Seen at the foot of the stem and in the tour's printer step from below and from the front; the topology-optimized bracket draws the same picture in the same time or less (the lattice 8.7 s, the mesher's last level 2.5 s). (`kernel/src/render/brep/dc/dc_tree.inl`, [docs/manufacturing.md](docs/manufacturing.md).)
- **A step that is still working says so, also while Show me does it; the texts beside a support, a load and a flow condition are in your language; what a card copies is readable.** The tour's note "FielDes is working on what this step shows. One moment." was not shown while a Show me ran, and the flow of chapter 9 takes twenty seconds cold (the beam of chapter 8 three): it is now. The labels in the picture of a support ("Fixed", "Sliding (fixed in x)"), a heat, a convection, an inlet, an outlet, a wall, a slip and gravity were built in English in every language: they go through the translation function now, with their 13 texts in the six tables (1781 of 1781). "Copy the text" of a result card put `maximum von Mises stress&nbsp;&nbsp;53.36&nbsp;MPamaximum displacement...` on the clipboard, one line with the entities as they were: a row of the card is a line now (`maximum von Mises stress 53.36 MPa`). Test verbs of the window for these tests: `closeasks`, `winrclick x y [menu]`, `clipboard`.
- **The tour teaches what a user does with the menus: faces by Select Surface, a shape from math, a foam, a cell of one's own, a symmetry with Shift+S.** The beam of the static analysis and the slab of the flow no longer come with boxes written for their ends: the end that is held and the end that is pulled, the face the water comes in at and the face it leaves by are **selected as faces** -- right-click the face in the viewport, Select Surface, Select -- and the support, the load, the inlet and the outlet are made from the face and the body with the right-click menu, as every user does (the faint input of the flow is hidden once the flow is drawn, because a see-through box over a result tinted it). New steps: **Points, edges and faces** (the corner, edge and face entries of the menu of a body, before the snapping), **A shape from math** and **Change the formula** (low_level_body: the formula of a shape written by hand and changed), **Points for a foam** and **The foam** (`random_points`, `voronoi_lattice`: a lattice that does not repeat), **A cell of your own** and **Change the equation** and **Fill the block with it** (`cell_custom_tpms` and a lattice made of it), and **Make a symmetry** (Shift+S with no symmetry in the script makes one and uses it) as the first step of the symmetry chapter; **Copy or link** is in the chapter of the objects (3), where the copies are, and no longer in the symmetry chapter. The two steps of the File menu (Preview as app, Export as app...) are done when the entry is chosen in the real menu -- the menu is drawn open with its entry lit, and the step is complete when the entry is clicked -- and the file dialog that the tour's own model made on "New" and on Quit is not asked (the tour owns the document it made). Every text is translated (six languages).
- **Lattices are in the Engineering menu; materials and fluids are one category.** *Periodic lattices* and *Non-periodic lattices* were under Operations -- but a lattice is made for a part, as an analysis is -- and are in Engineering now, after the domains (docs/lattices.md and the tour say the new path). *New material* and *New fluid* were two places apart (a loose entry and a category of Flow conditions); they are one category, **Materials**, with both.
- **The text of a card, of a note and of an error can be selected and copied.** The title, the text and the note of every card of the tour, the note of the model tree and the red banner of an error in the viewport were text you could look at but not take: they can be marked with the mouse and copied (Ctrl+C), and a right-click on a card or on the error banner offers **Copy the text** / **Copy the error**.
- **The tour's cards can be moved, the steps wait for the real thing, and the card says when it is working.** The line with the place in the tour (Chapter 3 of 12 · 4 / 15) is a handle: the card is dragged by it and stays where it is put until the step changes; a step that asks for a pane to be pulled out is complete when it is clicked again to put it back (while the pane is out the card has no arrow: its button is in the other window, and the arrow used to go to a speck at the corner of this one); the chapter-end card names the chapter that comes and what it is about. A step whose model takes a few seconds (a solved beam, a flow, a printer's result) says "FielDes is working on what this step shows. One moment." under its text instead of leaving an empty viewport. **Show me** starts from nothing selected, waits for the run that each of its clicks started and checks that every model it names is selected before it opens the menu: a Ctrl+click on a model that the step before had left selected took the selection off again. The tour's support, load, inlet and outlet are written as the menu writes them -- a face says where they act, so `body=None` and the shadow of the face alone -- so that what a user makes and what the tour goes on from are the same problem.
- **The solved problems of the tour come with the program.** The result cache (`seed-cache/result-cache` beside the program's `python` folder, read where it is) holds what the tour solves -- the beam, the flow, the optimizations -- so that the chapter shows a solved part at once, read from a file in a fraction of a second, where the first time took ten seconds with nothing to look at. A problem that is not in the user's own cache and is in the shipped one is read from it; everything else is as before. They are made by `scripts/make-seed-cache.ps1` for one version of the solvers (`RESULT_VERSION`) and copied into the packages by the build scripts; `FIELDES_NO_SEED=1` has none (to time a cold solve). ([docs/caching-and-performance.md](docs/caching-and-performance.md#the-result-cache).)
- **The foam and the cell of the tour are drawn in seconds.** A lattice of the diamond's cell, written as an equation, in a block of 28 x 28 x 20 took 50 s to draw; the block of the tour is 20 x 20 x 14, drawn at 4 samples to the millimetre. And the right-click entries `random_points`, `voronoi_lattice` and `delaunay_lattice` wrote a spacing of a tenth of the body (2 for that block: a thousand points, and a foam that takes minutes to draw); it is now between a third and a fifth of it (5 for that block: about a hundred and fifty points, the foam drawn in a few seconds), a number to change.
- **A flat overhang of a sharp box is coloured in `manufacturing_analysis`.** Found by the tour: the check colours the part at the corners of the mesh that is drawn, and an overhang rule leaves out the places near an edge (the normal there is a mix of two faces), so the underside of a box that hangs out, whose corners all lie on its edges, was drawn grey under a card that said "12 % of the surface breaks the overhang rule". The colour of a check is a class (red or not), and its triangles are now looked at inside as those of the change colour are: every triangle longer than 8 minimum features is cut, and every one whose corners differ is cut down to a four-thousandth of the part, and the check is read at the new corners. The underside is red, with the edge of the red where the check puts it; the numbers of the report were right all along and did not change. The cost is one more read of the check at the new corners: on the T of the tour 269 vertices became 47,247 in 365 ms (and 55,185 in 394 ms at the finer level), inside a drawing that took 3.3 s; the console prints it as `colour of a check, the big faces looked at inside`.
- **The flank of a gusset (and of a teardrop) is smooth: no more ribs.** The added gussets of `manufacturing_optimization` were drawn, from below, as ribs one lattice cell wide across a flank that should be a plane, with cream streaks through the red, and were exported the same. The cause, found by sampling the flank every 0.04 mm: the flank is cut out of the lattice a layer at a time, one column further out in every layer, by the pass that drops what rests on nothing, and the zero of the field between a node that was kept and one that was cut (set to a far value) fell a node's width apart from layer to layer -- a stair of one cell, 0.01 to 0.09 mm, with a slope of 0.987 where the flank is 1.0. The field is now smoothed as a surface: in a region of three nodes round every place where it changes sign the values are limited to a node and a half either way and blurred along x, y and z (twice each); an odd blur leaves a plane where it is and takes a step of a node away, and a crease is rounded within two nodes. Measured on the T part: the slope of the flank 0.9993 (1.0 is exact), its deviation from a straight line 2.5 microns rms where it was 24.5, and the pictures from below and from the front show one clean surface and a straight 45 degree line. It is in the exact field, so the drawn copy, the result and the exports are smooth alike. The render cache is renewed (version `fdrc5`).
- **The names of the arguments in the model tree are never cut.** The rows under a model (`a = (-5, -5, -5)`, `position`, `rotation`, `scale`, `alpha`, `Render settings`) had no icons or buttons, but were drawn in the first column only, which is narrow, and elided to `a...)`, with the space to the right left blank. A row with nothing in its other columns now spans the whole row, and its text is cut at the right end, never in the middle.
- **The cards of the guided tour are as wide as their row of buttons needs, and every button as wide as its text.** The card was a fixed 500 pixels, and with a larger font (or a longer language) the row of its five buttons -- Chapters, Quit the tour, Back, Show me, Skip step -- did not fit: the first letter and the arrow of "Skip step ▸" were cut off, because a button reported the width of a narrower, non-bold text before its style sheet applied. Each button is now at least as wide as its own text needs (in bold for the one that goes on), and the card as wide as the row; looked at in a window with the text at 100, 125 and 150 %.
- **The particles on the streamlines of a flow die the way they are born.** A particle was drawn as a short bright streak: released at the seed it grew out of it smoothly (the head leaves, the tail follows), but when its head came to the end of the line -- where the line leaves the fluid, at an outlet or at the edge of the region -- the whole streak was taken away at once. The head now stops at the end of the line and the tail runs on into it until the streak is gone, so a particle shrinks into the end of its line as it grew out of its seed (`Shape::drawFlowLines`).
- **A test window keeps its settings in one folder.** Every settings object of the program (the editor, the language, the shortcuts, the theme, the tour, the window) is made without names now, so `FIELDES_SETTINGS_DIR` really holds all of them: a settings object made with the program's names went to the registry whatever the variable said, and a test window wrote the tour's place into the user's registry.
- **A new model starts closed in the code editor too, not only in the model tree -- unless it has a placeholder.** The tree closed a model's row by default, but the editor's folds were only set from the choices you had saved, so a model you made (or a file you opened) was closed in the tree and open in the code. The editor's folds are now set to the rows of the tree after every change of it: a model nobody has opened or closed starts closed in both, the lines of its `handles(...)` / `place(...)` statements folded with its definition; a model with a placeholder to fill in starts open in both (as it did in the tree), and so does the one whose lines your text cursor is in, so what you are typing is not folded away. A line that the program writes into a model that is folded (the `handles(...)` line of a model made from a menu) no longer opens it: it is folded with the others.
- **A threaded rod made from the right-click menu is a rod.** The menu wrote `threaded_rod('M8', <a tenth of the view's span>, ...)`: the length followed the zoom, so zoomed in it was a few millimetres, a flat disc with hardly a turn of thread, while the diameter stayed the 8 mm of an M8. It is 20 mm long now (two and a half diameters, about sixteen turns), whatever the view.
- **The side of a threaded rod pulls its diameter the same way wherever it is pressed.** A surface is pulled along its own normal, and the flanks of a thread face up, down and out (and a drag along a tilted normal also leaves the part of the thread that the pull was started on): the same movement of the mouse did a different thing on each flank, and the pull was the same only on a crest. The rod now says where its axis is (`_pull_axis`; a placement's `rotation=` turns it with the rod), and a press on its side is pulled along the radius -- the part of the normal along the axis is not taken, so the arrow of the pull points straight away from the axis and the diameter follows the mouse the same on a crest, a root and both flanks, on both sides. The ends of the rod keep pulling along it. (A rod inside a union or another combination has no axis of its own to name: its side is pulled by the combination's normal, as before.) And for a test window: `FIELDES_SETTINGS_DIR` makes the program keep its settings in an .ini file in that folder, and read and write nothing of the user's.
- **The half space is out of the Parametric shapes menu; the side of a threaded rod pulls its diameter.** `half_space` has no size, no handles and no picture of its own -- it is the solid on one side of a plane, with no end -- so as a menu entry nobody could tell what it was, and a horizontal one drew nothing (the mesher merged every cell of an exactly flat surface into one, and one cell has no faces; a cell on the boundary of the region is no longer merged into one bigger than a few cells, so a surface that leaves through the boundary is drawn up to it -- in dual contouring, the default, and in iso-simplex; the hybrid mesher never merges). The plane as a surface (New surface > plane) and the cut of a body by a plane (Operations > Combining > cut_plane) are in the menus; `half_space(norm, point)` stays in the library for scripts, explained: what `intersection` and `difference` cut a body by, and a region of a mesh zone, a support or a load. Pulling the side of a `threaded_rod` did nothing (its diameter came from the screw's name and was no number): the diameter is a handle now, the first of the three numbers `handles()` writes (the diameter, then the two ends) -- the pitch stays, so the thread keeps its profile. A rod whose `handles()` line has two numbers needs it written again: delete the line (or Reset it in the model tree) and pull again.
- **S puts a body onto another by two points again.** Selecting two points on two bodies and pressing S said "none of the features says which body it is on" and moved nothing: a point that is selected is wrapped in `place(...)` so that its gizmo can move it, and that copy did not carry the body the point was made on. The copy carries it now (and a snap follows a placed copy back to the point it came from), so the first point's body is moved onto the second's.
- **A primitive of your own is a file, and the model it makes is one line.** *Add selection* pasted every statement the model was made of into the script, and the model tree could only show the last one: the expansion and what belongs to one entity were lost. A primitive is now a function in a file of its own, `<name>.py` in the `primitives` folder of your data folder (a block like any other: the editor completes it and goes to its definition), and picking it writes ONE line, `name_1 = name()` -- the model is one object in the script and in the tree, drag adjustable and movable as any, and what it is made of stays in its file, as the geometry of an imported part stays in its cache files. The numbers it was made with are plain numbers in the file (a `var()` of the script it came from is its number), and the placement arguments the model carried at their defaults are left out. A script that calls a primitive needs its file, as a script that imports a file needs that file; removing a primitive deletes its file. The primitives an older `primitives.json` held are written as files once. An element of the window that is pulled out into a window of its own (the editor, the viewport, the console, the tree, a card) no longer leaves an empty placeholder with a *Put it back* button: the panes round it take its room, and it goes back from its own corner or by closing its window.
- **The default mesh is at most about 100,000 tetrahedra, and Engineering > Mesh writes the body alone.** `mesh_volume(body)` with no `element_size` took about 40 elements along the longest side up to half a million tetrahedra: a plain 10 mm cube was 506,328 tetrahedra and 85 seconds, and more than the 400,000 the viewer draws, so the card said the elements were not shown. The budget is 100,000 now (the same cube: 107,000 tetrahedra, 21 s, the elements drawn and the quality numbers made; a long thin part keeps its 40 along the side). And the right-click entries `mesh_volume` and `mesh_surface` wrote one placeholder for every argument (13 and 8): the menu took every parameter of a function without conditions for a model it is made for; they write `mesh_volume(box_1)` now, every other argument has its default. `alpha=` was written and shown in the tree and drawn solid: it is drawn see-through now (0 is a ghost at 5 %, not "not drawn"). The result of a solver written in Python starts with its elements drawn, each coloured by its value (it was one colour, the mean). Escape cancels a render that is running and leaves a finished picture alone (it took every finished shape out of the viewport).
- **`capsule` and `slot` are shapes, not fields.** `capsule` was written as `distance_to_segment(...) - r`, and arithmetic on a distance field is a field: the model tree gave it a field's icon and no gizmo. It is now a body whose field is the exact distance to its surface (a 3D shape with its gizmo), and `slot` is a 2D shape; every function of the CAD module also says what it makes before it has run (`_makes`), and the checks ask the tree's own question -- the kind a statement makes and whether it has a gizmo -- of every one of them.
- **The new base shapes are drag adjustable, a face each.** Dragging the slot only scaled it: `capsule`, `tube`, `slot` and `threaded_rod` were plain fields with no numbers that stand for a face, and a shape with none can only be scaled by a pull. Each is made of face numbers now, as `rectangle` is: a capsule has its radius (the side) and the heights of its ends, a tube its outer wall, its bore and its two ends, a slot its four sides (an end pulled makes it longer and keeps the round end, a long side pulled makes it wider and keeps both ends round), a threaded rod its diameter (its side: the pitch stays, so the thread keeps its profile) and its two ends. `handles()` makes the shape again from the numbers a drag gives it, like every primitive; `tests/internal/cad_checks.py` pulls each face and checks that it moves that face only.
- **A number of any CAD function may be a `var()`.** `rotate`, `reflect`, `mirror`, `linear_pattern` (the step), `circular_pattern` (the angle), `revolve` (the angle, the point of the axis), `cut_plane`, `hole` (position, diameter, depth, head sizes), `capsule`, `tube`, `slot` and `align` build the body from the number as a field, so a gizmo or a handle drags it and the body follows. A count and the direction of a hole stay plain numbers (a different count is a different body, not a moved one).
- **`revolve` turns a profile on either side of the axis, or across it, by any angle.** A partial turn needed the profile on the right of the axis; the part on the left now turns the same way round the axis (toward -z), a profile that crosses the axis is cut into its two halves and each is turned, and a `var()` angle works (a turn that stops at the angle).
- **Holes for screws: `hole(plate, position, screw='M6', kind='counterbore')`.** `screw` (M2 to M24, `pitch=` for a fine thread) gives the sizes: the clearance hole (ISO 273, `fit='medium'` or `'close'`), the room of a socket head cap screw for a counterbore (ISO 4762 head + 1 mm across, + 0.5 mm deep), of a 90 degree flat head for a countersink, and for `kind='tapped'` the tap drill; `modeled=True` makes the thread itself (an ISO profile, right-hand or `hand='left'`, over `thread_depth`). `screw_spec('M6')` says the numbers, `threaded_rod('M8', 30)` is the external thread, a rod that fits. A modelled thread asks for a mesh fine enough for its pitch.
- **`distance_between` looks at every place two bodies come near each other.** The closest pair of vertices can belong to the wrong place when the mesh is coarse; now the closest pairs in up to eight different places are each improved by the projection and the shortest is the distance.
- **The documentation says how `mirror` relates to the Symmetry feature.** `reflect` and `mirror` are one-off operations on a body (the result is a finished body with no link); a symmetry (`symmetry_planes`, Shift+S) is the live version whose images follow every edit. The Limits section of [docs/cad.md](docs/cad.md) is rewritten to hold only what cannot be built.
- **Settings → Units.** Length (mm, cm, m, in, ft, um), force (N, kN, lbf, kgf), stress and pressure (MPa, GPa, kPa, Pa, bar, psi, ksi), mass (kg, g, t, lb) and temperature (C, K, F) are chosen once: the ruler, the key numbers of a result card and the numbers of its colour bar and legends, the value under the cursor, the position of the cursor, the work plane's step, offset and readout and the radius of a surface selection are shown in them; a mesh file is written in the length unit (`export_mesh(..., unit='in')` overrides it, `Mesh.export` takes it as the default `scale`; 3MF, DXF and FBX say the unit, a glTF stays in metres), and a mesh file that does not say what its numbers mean is read in it (`file_units=` overrides). The program itself, the scripts and the solvers keep mm, N, MPa, kg and degrees Celsius, so a script means the same on every machine. (`python/fieldes/units.py`, `app/include/fieldes/units.hpp`, `tests/internal/units_checks.py`.)
- **Settings → Solver: the equations of the analyses, and solvers of your own in Python.** The dialog lists the analyses FielDes solves in C++ (with their equations), the solvers of the library (`heat`, and `elasticity` -- the equations of the static analysis in Python, with a stiffness that may depend on the strain) and the Python files of your solvers folder. **Make an editable copy** of a library solver to change its equations, **Add from a file...** to bring in a solver written by somebody else, **New solver...** to start from a working one; **Reset to default** puts the text a solver started with (the library solver it was copied from, the template, or the file as added) back in the editor -- nothing is written until *Save and check*, and Ctrl+Z undoes it --, and the text before every save is kept as `name.py.bak` (**Previous version**); `solve('name', part, ...)` meshes the part, runs the solver on it and draws the fields it found like any result. See [docs/solvers.md](docs/solvers.md); `tests/internal/solver_checks.py` checks the library solvers against bars with exact answers.
- **Help → Validation and accuracy.** One page, [docs/validation.md](docs/validation.md), with everything every kind of analysis was checked against and the accuracy that was found, by analysis (static, modal, thermal, flow, the optimisations, manufacturing, meshing, CAD, your own solvers), and what is not validated.
- **Placement is four arguments of the call -- `position`, `rotation`, `scale`, `alpha` -- and the tree shows them under every object.** The `gizmo(...)` line is gone: where an object is, how it is turned, how big and how opaque it is are arguments of its own call (`leg = cylinder(2, 10, position=(5, 0, 0), rotation=(0, 90, 0))`), and the gizmo in the viewport drags those numbers; a symmetry takes `position` and `rotation` (it has no scale). Under every object that has a gizmo the tree shows the four rows, always: the ones the call writes with their values, the others dimmed with their defaults and a tooltip that says so; a click on a dimmed row writes the four into the call and selects the value in the code editor. Objects made from a menu start closed. (`fieldes/stdlib/placement.py`, [docs/scripting.md](docs/scripting.md#placement-where-an-object-is-how-it-is-turned-how-big-and-how-see-through).)
- **Any major element can be pulled out into a window of its own.** The code editor, the console and output, the viewport, the model tree and every card (section, field viewer, work plane, symmetry, result, live view) have a small button in their top right corner: pressed, the element goes into a window that can be put on another screen, with a placeholder and a *Put it back* button in its place; pressed again, or with the window closed, it goes back where it was. The window keeps the shortcuts of the window it came from, remembers its place and size, and an element that was out when FielDes ended is pulled out again at the next start. The viewport is the one that is a graphics widget: its graphics resources are let go of and made again in the new window (`View::releaseGL`). See [docs/interface.md](docs/interface.md#windows-of-their-own). (`app/src/popout.cpp`, `app/include/fieldes/popout.hpp`.)
- **Navigation: *Left drag turns* and *Custom...*.** Settings → Navigation has two entries more: *Left drag turns* (a drag of the left button on empty space turns the view, Alt + left drag pans, a click still selects and Ctrl + drag still draws the rectangle) and *Custom...*, which chooses the gesture of turning, panning and zooming -- a mouse button with Shift, Ctrl or Alt held. What is not chosen stays as it is for everyone.

### A support holds where you say; two selection bugs

- **`fixed(region, displacement=(0, 0, 0))`: which directions are held, and where.** The three flags `x=`, `y=`, `z=` are gone: `displacement` is a vector of three numbers (mm). **0** (the default) lets the part not move in that direction, **`inf`** (`math.inf`, `float('inf')`; `from fieldes import *` has it as `inf`) leaves it free, so `fixed(region, displacement=(0, 0, inf))` is a support the part slides along z on, and **any other number is a displacement the support imposes** -- `(0, 0, -0.5)` pushes the part 0.5 mm down where it is held, and the static analysis solves for the rest of it (the solver takes the imposed movement out of the loads of the free directions and gives it back to the held ones; both the linear and the 10-node element). A part that is only pushed by its supports -- a settling support, a prescribed deflection -- needs no force: an imposed displacement counts as the load (the analysis said "no loads" until it did), and the **support reaction** is then the force the displaced supports push with (a cantilever held at one end and pushed 1 mm at the other reports the force of that push; the net of all supports is zero when only displacements load the part), and the compliance the work that force does. A modal analysis holds the part still, a topology optimization refuses an imposed displacement and says what to do instead, the voxel solver likewise. The card says "Fixed, displaced z -0.5 mm"; the exported Abaqus file has the value on its `*BOUNDARY` line and the Nastran file a `SPC` card for each node. The kernel: `libfive_tetfea_add_support(f, region, x, y, z, dx, dy, dz)`.
- **`custom_resolution(body, r)` is the number the scene's resolution is, for one body -- and its number is an argument like any other.** It was an absolute number of samples per mm, while the scene's resolution is scaled by the size of what is drawn (a small body in a big region is drawn finer than the number says), so to get a body as fine as the scene's own picture a number far from the scene's had to be typed (500 for a small cone). Now `custom_resolution(cone, 10)` in a scene at 10 is the cone as it was, twice as much is twice as fine, and the body is scaled by its size as the scene's resolution is (at the most 2000 samples along its longest side; an imported part follows the same number). The row under the model in the tree is not a field to type in any more: it reads `custom_resolution = 10`, and a click selects the number in the code, ready to be typed over -- as every argument of a call does. `bounds=` of the function is gone (the extent is not needed any more), and so are the automation verbs `setting`, `settingtype` and `fieldfocus` that typed into the field.
- **The tip of a cone is rounded by half a percent of its base radius.** The apex of a perfect cone is a point the surface comes to from every side at once, and a mesh has to place a vertex for each cell round it: it came out ragged -- and the cone looks the same near its tip at every scale, so a finer resolution did not help. A tip rounded by a small share of the radius is the same cone at the scale of the cells that are coarser than it and is smooth for the cells that are finer (`cone`, in `fieldes/stdlib/shapes.py`; its two faces and its handles are as they were).
- **A boundary condition takes the BODY it acts on, and bodies for where.** `fixed(plate, body=bracket)` holds the bracket on its surface where it meets the plate; the call makes the surface (`surface_from_bodies(bracket, plate)`) itself, so nothing is intersected or cut out by hand to give a condition a place. `body=` is an argument of `fixed`, `force`, `fixed_temperature`, `heat_input`, `convection`, `inlet`, `outlet`, `wall` and `slip`; a single surface (or a field) is still a region by itself. The menus write it: select the body first and the plates after it, and the entry is written `fixed(region=plate, body=bracket)` -- two bodies used to be written as `region=[bracket, plate]`, the union of both, which held the whole bracket.
- **Ctrl + click on a spline, a polyline or the edge of a profile adds it to the selection.** A line has no surface in the picture the viewport reads clicks from, so the press with Ctrl held (which skips the "pull a point out of the outline" test of a plain click) found no model at all and did nothing: with something else selected, the curve could not be added.
- **A new profile, polyline or spline made on empty space is made of what is selected, not of the last model of the script.** The menu fell back to "the last model" when nothing was selected -- right for an operation, wrong for a shape -- so with one spline in the script a new profile was written `profile(spline_1)`.
- **`Ctrl+Shift+W` with a surface selected makes the work plane on it.** A selected face (a patch on the surface of a body) gives its own plane, through the middle of the face, with its normal turned to the side you look from; a surface that is not flat gets the plane that covers the most of it -- the direction its triangles, weighted by their areas, face the most (`Shape::patchPlane`). A 2D shape or three selected points still give theirs first, and with nothing selected the plane is parallel to the view.
- **A click on a render setting marks its number for replacement.** *Render settings* in the tree (Region, Resolution, Quality) went to the line of the `view.set_*` call and no further; now the number is selected in the code, ready to be typed over, as a click on a variable or an argument does -- the region selects both corners (`(lo), (hi)`). A setting the script does not have yet (shown dimmed) has no number to select. The same click on the `custom_resolution` row of a model had the same flaw (the early return for setting rows came before the code that selects the number): fixed with it.
- **Right-click a body near an edge or a corner: a point or a line there.** The menu of a body gained, below *Select Surface* and only where it is true, **Point at the corner**, **Point on the edge** and **Line along the edge** (for a straight edge: one that a corner closes at each end -- the rim of a cylinder is an edge and gets a point, not a line). Each writes a call under the body -- `corner_at(part, (x, y, z))`, `edge_point_at(...)`, `edge_at(...)`, new in `fieldes/stdlib/features.py`, with the place the library found (the corner, the point of the edge) and not the click -- that finds the feature from the body's field (the surface round the click is sampled, its normals are sorted into faces, two faces that meet at a sharp angle are an edge, three a corner; the place is where their planes meet, worked out again from smaller and smaller neighbourhoods, and only a jump of the normal that stays a jump when looked at closely counts, so a sphere, the side of a cylinder or a fillet has none). It returns an ordinary `point` / `polyline`, and follows the body when the body changes. See [Selecting surfaces](docs/selecting-surfaces.md#corners-and-edges).
- **An object is one block in the editor, and told from the next by a shade.** The arrow in the gutter of a model's definition folded the definition with its `gizmo`, `handles`, `lock`, `render_cache` and `custom_resolution` lines, but not with the line that shows it (`part` on a line of its own, which FielDes writes under every model it creates): that line now goes on with its model, in the fold and in the block, and so does the `# hidden: part` line of a model that is not shown. A definition that runs over several lines (`part = sphere(` ... `)`) folds up to its closing bracket and takes the rest of the model with it: the fold used to stop at the last indented line of the definition, which left the closing bracket and every line that goes on with the model (its handles, its gizmo, the line that shows it) open under the arrow. Every second object also has a slightly different background -- a shade of the editor's own paper, lighter in the dark theme and darker in the light one -- so that where one definition starts and ends is seen (a number, a `var(...)` and the plain functions of Python are not objects and are not shaded). `Script::updateBlockShading`, a layer of the editor's extra selections; made again a moment after the text stops changing and when the theme changes.
- **`Delete` deletes the selected models, and `Esc` leaves the mode you are in.** The shortcut of *Delete the selected models* was the letter `D` alone; the `Delete` key does it too now (viewport or model tree focused, so it never eats typing). `Esc` was only "cancel render" -- and the work plane did not listen to it at all, because the window's shortcut took the key before the viewport saw it. Now `Esc` goes one step out of the innermost special mode: the line being drawn on the work plane is dropped, then the kind of drawing that is on stops (clicks select again), then the work plane view closes, then the section view closes; the console closes on `Esc` when its line has the keyboard; with none of those on it cancels the render, as before (`View::exitMode`).
- **The output pane is a terminal: output above, a `>>>` line for Python at its foot, always there.** The output pane could only show; now the line at its foot (`Ctrl+\`` or View → Console puts the cursor in it; the splitter above the pane sets its height, which is remembered) takes Python and runs it among the names of the script that ran last and of the library -- `bracket.bounds`, `volume_of(bracket)`, `help(select_surface)`, a block of several lines (the value of its last expression is shown, as in a notebook), with `Enter` to run, `Shift+Enter` for a new line and `Up`/`Down` through what was run. It runs on a thread of its own (a long computation does not stop the window) in a copy of the script's names: what the console assigns is its own and kept between commands, and the script and its tree are not touched. A traceback shows from the console's line on, in red. (`terminal_exec` in `fieldes/app_support.py`; the test verb `console`.)
- **Snap: select the faces, edges and points on two bodies and press `S` -- the first body is put onto the other, and nothing new is made.** The body is turned by the least turn that makes its face face the other one (the direction in which the two do not go into one another) and moved so that its point is on the other point; a second face or edge on each lines them up about the contact, edges alone are made parallel, a place alone is only moved. The move is written where the gizmo writes one -- into the body's own `position=` and `rotation=`, on top of the placement it has, as `var()` numbers its gizmo drags -- so the mesh that is drawn is carried to its new place: no new model, no new calculation, one undo takes it back. The places the faces, edges and points on that body were made at (`select_surface(bracket, seed=...)`, `corner_at(bracket, ...)`) are moved with it in the same edit, so they stay on the bracket; one whose place is not written as numbers is reported and left. (`ScenePanel::snapSelected`, `snap_placement` in `fieldes/app_support.py`, `fieldes/stdlib/snapping.py`. There is no `snap(...)` function or Operations entry: a snap is the move of a body, not a model. The first form, `snap_1 = snap(...)`, made a new field and hid the body: it re-meshed what was only displaced.) See [Scripting](docs/scripting.md#snapping-a-body-onto-another).
- **A model that stands exactly on the faces of the default region is drawn.** The region a script gets when it sets none is -10 to 10 on every axis, and a cube from -10 to 10 had its faces on the edge of it, where the mesher finds no change of sign: nothing was drawn, with no word about it. A model that reaches the default region is now made room for like one that is past it (`view.set_bounds(...)` is written with the room, as it is for a model that is larger). (`ScenePanel` fits the region to the models; seen in the window t362 before the change, the cube is missing from every picture.)
- **A dark theme, and the colours by hand: Settings → Theme.** The window was light (white bars, a pale warm ground, a paper-white editor) around a dark viewport; now **Dark** is a second set -- a dark teal window, the editor in Solarized's darkest base, the line numbers, tabs, scroll bars, dialogs, fields and buttons with it -- and each of the eight colours of either set (**Window**, **Bars and buttons**, **Editor**, **Line numbers**, **Lines**, **Text**, **Quiet text**, **Selection and focus**) can be chosen by hand, with a **Default** for each and **Reset colours** for the set. The two sets keep their own choices; the shades that follow from the eight (a field's border, a pressed button, the selection of the editor, the page of a file that is not run, the scroll bar handles, the hover of the editor's buttons) are worked out from them, so they follow a choice. It changes what is open as it is chosen (`Theme::apply`, `Editor::applyTheme`) and is kept in the settings (`theme/mode`, `theme/<set>/<colour>`). Light is as it was.

### The elements of every result can be seen and cut; topology_optimization is on top of the Engineering menu; every primitive has an icon of its own

- **A thermal result, a flow and the modes of a modal analysis show their elements, and the section viewer cuts through them.** Only a static result handed the viewer its element mesh (the *Elements* button of the result card, *Whole elements* in the section card), so the other results had neither. Now the thermal result (the temperature, and the heat flux of each element), the flow (the speed of each element, its pressure) and every mode of a modal analysis (the displacement of the mode, its value in each element) are drawn on their tetrahedra as the static result is: the same button, the same cut through whole elements. The kernel gives the element mesh and the values of each (`libfive_tetthermal_mesh`, `libfive_tetflow_mesh`, `libfive_tetfea_mode_node_displacements` and `..._element_values`), `fieldes/stdlib/element_mesh.py` builds what the viewer takes.
- **A meshing suite: `mesh_volume`, `mesh_surface`, and the mesh written for the solver of another program.** A body is meshed with tetrahedra (`element='tet'`), 10-node tetrahedra (`'tet10'`, the edges on the surface curved) or hexahedra (`'hex'`: every tetrahedron cut into four), by the isosurface stuffing the analyses use -- the kernel's mesher, which Python did not use before -- with an `element_size` that is a number or **a field** (a body whose value is the size, a function of a list of points, or `graded_size(body, wall_size, growth_rate, max_size)`: the longest edge of a tetrahedron is bisected, with those round it, until the edges are as short as the field asks, the new boundary vertices are put on the surface), and the poor tetrahedra are **mended** (`optimize=True`: the nodes of poor tetrahedra move toward the middle of their neighbours -- along the surface for those on it -- and edges and faces are swapped and interior nodes collapsed where that makes the worst tetrahedron round them better; example 36 has 0.35 % of its cells above skewness 0.85 and none above 0.95 (the worst is 0.92), where the tetrahedra as the bisection leaves them have 3 % above 0.85 and 160 above 0.95), **prism layers** on the surface of a solid for a flow (`boundary_layers=inflation_layers(body, first_height, growth_rate, layers)`: the core is the body less that thickness, the layers are its boundary moved to the wall in steps), **named zones** (`zones={'inlet': region, 'outlet': region}`, the rest `wall`, split at the creases with `feature_angle`; a strip narrower than two edges, the rim where prisms meet a plane or the edge a lattice cut off, joins its neighbour instead of being a zone of its own), cell zones, and `mesh.quality().report()` -- skewness, orthogonal quality, aspect ratio, volume ratio, the smallest dihedral angle, each with what a solver wants. `mesh.export(path)` / `export_mesh(mesh, path)` writes Gmsh (`.msh`), VTK (`.vtu`), Abaqus (`.inp`), Nastran (`.bdf`), I-DEAS universal (`.unv`), Ansys Fluent (`format='fluent'`), OpenFOAM (`format='openfoam'`: a polyMesh folder, the zones as patches), STL and OBJ; the cells of the Fluent and OpenFOAM files are checked closed. A mesh drawn on its own has a card whose *Elements* show every cell coloured by its size or its quality. The Engineering menu has a **Mesh** category with both calls. (`python/fieldes/stdlib/meshing.py` and `meshwriters.py`, [docs/meshing.md](docs/meshing.md), example 36.)
- **CAD operations: turn about any axis, mirror in any plane, patterns, revolve, cut by a plane, holes, align, measure.** `rotate(body, axis, angle, center)` (degrees, any axis through any point), `reflect` and `mirror(body, normal, point)` (the image, or the body and its image), `linear_pattern(body, count, step)` and `circular_pattern(body, count, axis, center, angle)`, `revolve(profile, angle, axis_point, axis_direction)` (about any line of the profile's plane, the whole turn or a part), `cut_plane` and `split` (a body cut by a plane), `hole` and `hole_cutter` (a hole from a point in any direction, simple, counterbored or countersunk), `capsule`, `tube`, `slot`, `align(body, to, axis, mode, gap)` and the measurements `surface_area`, `section_area`, `interference`, `distance_between`. They are in the right-click menus (Moving, Repeating, Extruding, Combining, and the parametric shapes) and each is checked against a volume, an area or a point that is known exactly by `tests/internal/cad_checks.py`. (`python/fieldes/stdlib/cad.py`, [docs/cad.md](docs/cad.md), example 37.)
- **More to snap to: the centre of a circle, the centre of a face, the corner and the edge that a rounding replaced.** `circle_center_at(body, near)` is the centre of the circular edge nearest `near` (the rim of a cylinder or a hole, the base of a cone), read from the edge itself at wider and wider angles round it; `face_center(face)` is the middle of a selected face (the mean of its sample points: the centre of a disc, the centre of the area of an L); `corner_at(...)` and `edge_point_at(...)` take `rounded=True` and find, for an edge that is rounded, the point where the flat faces on both sides meet. The right-click menu of a body offers them (*Point at the centre of this face* on any surface; *Point at the centre of the circle*, *Point at the rounded corner*, *Point on the rounded edge* near the edge or the rounding, only where it is true), so the bottom of a cylinder can be snapped onto the base of a cone, on one axis, by the centres of their circles. (`python/fieldes/stdlib/features.py`, `tests/internal/snap_checks.py`, [docs/selecting-surfaces.md](docs/selecting-surfaces.md).)
- **The default element size follows the geometry: a solid part does not get millions of elements.** The default was the part's longest side over 60 (40 for modal, thermal, flow and topology analyses, half of that for the 10-node elements), which for a part that is solid -- a cube -- is a great many tetrahedra: a cube 0.4 mm on a side had 2 678 400 and took 18.6 s. The default is now that size or, when the tetrahedra it would make are more than a budget -- a million for a static and a thermal analysis, 500 000 for a modal one, 400 000 for a topology optimisation, 300 000 for a flow, a quarter of those for the 10-node elements --, the size at which the part has that many, estimated from its volume (`fieldes/stdlib/element_size.py`: a coarse sample of the part's box and about twelve tetrahedra to the cube of an element). A thin or slender part (a plate, a lattice, a shell) has little volume beside its box and is not changed; an `element_size` that is given is used as it is, with no limit. Seen in the window t365 on his case (the cube, held at its bottom, 1 N across its top): 1 063 776 tetrahedra and 192 273 nodes in 5.7 s, the Elements button there; the estimate was 1.0 million. The peak stress of a part held at an edge is a number of the mesh (66.6 MPa here, 55.8 MPa on the finer mesh): refine `element_size` where it matters.
- **The elements of a large mesh are drawn, and a result with too many says so.** The *Elements* button was missing from the card of every result with more than 1 500 000 tetrahedra, without a word -- and the default mesh of a solid part is that large (the largest extent over 60: a cube 0.4 mm on a side had 2 678 400 tetrahedra and 475 381 nodes, 18.6 s and a 150 MB result file): that was the missing button of a small box with a force on it. The limit is 8 000 000 now, and above it the card says "N tetrahedra: more than the 8 000 000 that can be drawn, so the elements are not shown" (six translations).
- **`topology_optimization` is the first entry of the Engineering menu, on its own.** It was a line of Engineering > Structural, although it designs for an analysis of any domain -- a static one, a modal one, a thermal one, a flow. It is above the categories now, with a line under it (`kinds.py`, `menu_catalog.py`, `fillEngineeringMenu`, the golden menu file).
- **Every primitive has an icon of its own, in the menus and in the model tree.** Every entry of *Primitives*, *Parametric shapes*, the lattice cells and the Engineering menus, and every row of the tree that is one, had the picture of its kind: a sphere, a cylinder, a cone and a torus were the same cube. Now a sphere is a ball, a cylinder a cylinder, a cone a cone, a torus a ring, a pyramid and a prism what they are; a circle, a rectangle, a ring, a polygon and a triangle are their outlines; a plane, a sphere surface and a cylinder surface are a sheet, a globe and a tube; the fields are rings round a point, shells round an axis, layers of height, noise and waves; a support is a wall with its hatching, a force an arrow, gravity a falling ball, heat an arrow into a wall, a flame in a block, a circulating arrow, an inlet and an outlet arrows through a line, a wall and a slip wall arrows along it; a metal is an ingot with its element on it, a plastic a leaf, a bottle, a brick, a fibre; a fluid a drop, a drop of oil, a flask, curling air; and each of the 28 cells of a lattice is its own picture (the cube of struts it repeats in, a section of its surface, or its pattern). What has none -- anything that is not a primitive -- keeps the picture of its kind. The tree's rows know the primitive from the call (`cell_periodic('gyroid')` is the gyroid, `Material('PLA', ...)` is PLA).
- **The measured mesh dependence of a peak stress is in the documentation.** The peak von Mises stress of the bracket of example 05 (2 mm linear elements) reads 58 to 67 MPa with the mesh lattice at four places and 41 MPa on a fifth, while its displacement stays within 0.6 %: `docs/analysis.md` says so, and that the safety factor of the example reads between 1.4 and 1.6 (2.3 on the mesh of an earlier version) until the element size is halved or `element='tet10'` is asked for.

### Topology optimization takes the analysis: any number it measures is the goal or a limit

- **`topology_optimization(analysis, constraints=[...])`** replaces `topology_optimization(part, supports, loads, volume_fraction=...)`: the problem is the analysis it is given (its part is the design space, its supports, loads and material are what the design is made for), and what the design is for is one list, as in `parametric_optimization` -- `minimize(x)` / `maximize(x)` (at most one; none: find a design that keeps the conditions) and comparisons in a text (`'volume_fraction <= 0.3'`, `'max_von_mises <= 90'`, `'frequencies[0] == 100'`, `'80 <= frequencies[0] <= 120'`). Any of the compliance, the mass, the volume, the peak stress, the safety factor, the largest displacement, the displacement at a place and the natural frequencies can be the goal or a limit, alone or in a formula (`'mass + 0.01 * compliance'`), from a static and a modal analysis at once (`[stress, vibration]`). `parametric_optimization(analysis, variables, constraints=[...])` has the same shape; `minimize=` / `maximize=` arguments are gone from both. **Several load cases are a list of analyses** (each simulated completely separately, the design made for all of them); several loads in one analysis act together.
- **One optimiser for all of it.** Every measure is calculated with its derivative to the density of every element (the compliance and the frequencies from the solution, the displacement and the stress with one more solve each), the formulas are differentiated, and the design is moved by the method of moving asymptotes on the goal and every condition (`kernel/src/fea/topopt.cpp`, `TetProblem::optimize`); the sensitivities were checked against two evaluations of the measure either side of the design (six digits for the compliance, the volume, the displacements and the frequency). A step that makes the design worse (by the merit: the goal plus what the conditions are broken by) is taken back and halved, the sharpening of the density waits while a condition is broken, and the design given back is the best of the last, crisp stage.
- **The stress limit.** The element's stress is relaxed at low density (a grey element reports its stress times its density to the power `penalty - 2.5`, so that a foam of grey elements costs more material than a crisp layout), no element reports less than the void's stiffness gives it (so that a thin link is not taken down to nothing unseen), and the peak is the p-norm of them brought to the peak. The part that is cut from the design is analysed again, and its own peak is the one that is reported.
- **The optimised part is one piece that can be analysed.** It is cut from the design at the density that keeps its volume; if that leaves several pieces, or members thinner than the mesh of the analysis can follow (the supports do not hold the part, a load does not touch it), the cut is lowered a little at a time to the first density at which it is one piece that can be analysed, and the output says which cut it used and how much of the part it keeps, next to the design's own share. The analysis of that part is made for you (`best.analysis`, `best.values`, `best.feasible`), and the output table gives what was asked at the first iteration, at the last and as the optimised part has it.
- **Loose material is held only when it joins an element that holds a support** (the slab under a load holds nothing itself).
- **The density filter smooths the design only.** An element that is held -- solid (round a support or a load, a `keep` region) or empty (`avoid`) -- no longer lends its density to the design elements within the filter radius: it put density into them whatever the stress there said, and material that no stress reached stayed beside every held region (the corner of a grown flange beside a support plate, the rungs along a plate in the live view). `runDensityDesign` in `density_design.cpp`; the numbers of every topology optimisation with a support, a load or a kept region change a little.
- **`grow` has no limit.** `grow=True`: the design is not limited to the part at all -- it starts as the part, puts material anywhere and takes it away anywhere, wherever that makes it best (`volume_fraction` is of the part's own volume; nothing is added behind the face of the part that a support or a load acts on -- over the face (a column of space in front of it: the hole the mesh has, so that the face is the one surface a support holds and a load acts on), and past the plane of the face a few elements round its outline (its elements are held empty, and the part is cut within it), where the wall (or the body that pushes) is (`_face_columns` in `fea.py`: found by sampling the part inside the places, a face is the outermost sample of a column with no part a little farther out, flat, and a good part of the largest; its plane found to a thousandth of an element) -- and nothing else is forbidden, on the part's side of the plane, beside the plate. **A support holds the face only** (`libfive_tetfea_set_condition_surface`: the nodes on the surface of the mesh, as the loads act on boundary faces only), not the slab of the part behind it that the region of the support takes in: `fixed(box)` with a box that overlaps the part by a millimetre held that slab and its side strips, and a design that may grow was rewarded for material curled round the plate's edge, past the plane of the face, clamped to held nodes for nothing -- and forbidding that with a zone of any shape (the box a place was drawn as, a half-space behind the plane) only made a flat wall and a notch that no stress asked for (a place with no flat face, a bolt hole that carries a load, is kept clear as the place itself); the supports and loads act on the part itself and never on the walls that the grown design space has round those places -- `libfive_tetfea_set_condition_body`: a node farther outside the part than half an element is not held, a boundary face with a corner there is not loaded; every design space of a run is meshed on the lattice of the first, so that the thin hole a place has beside the part is resolved in all of them (a mesh that bridged it with tetrahedra drew as a comb of triangles in front of a support plate and held material to the part that is not the part's); before, a free design built a cap round the support, fixed to the ground, and took a share of the load on the wall of the hole). The numeric `grow=3.0` (a shell of a fixed thickness round the whole part, all of it in the equations whether the design used it or not) is gone. **The design space follows the design**: the part and a thin skin round it first; while material reaches the edge of the space outside the part, the space is made again -- the part, its skin and everything within four elements of the material, as the exact distance to the surface of the design -- and the SAME run goes on in it, with the design, the sharpening stage, the step size and the iteration it had (the run stops at the end of the iteration in which material first stands in an element of the edge: `libfive_tetfea_set_stop_at_edge`, `libfive_tetfea_resume`, `libfive_tetfea_design`, `libfive_tetfea_set_start_field`, `libfive_tetfea_set_start_domain`, `libfive_tetfea_set_resume`; `topology_optimization` in `fea.py`), so the design is never held against a wall while it runs, a design that does not reach the edge costs what it always did, and one that does costs what its material costs. Only the iterations stop the growth (if they run out with the design still at the edge of its space, the output says so and the part is cut there). A restriction is an additional argument, never the base: `grow_limit=<mm>` is the most the design may reach out.
- **The optimised part is cut at the density that keeps the design's volume, counted exactly.** It was counted on a grid of 40 samples to an axis, which cannot see a thin wall: the angle bracket `Bracket.step` (about 5 mm walls) asked for half of its material came out with 38 % (41 % with the exact count: the plate of that bracket is grey through its thickness, and a surface that lies in a plateau of the density is not followed by the mesh of the analysis at that element size). The kernel counts the volume of the nodal density above a level in each tetrahedron (the field is linear in it) and finds the level that has the volume of the design (`TetProblem::cutThreshold`).
- **A body in a flow is designed by the same call.** `topology_optimization(fluid_analysis(body, domain, ...), constraints=[minimize('drag_force'), 'volume_fraction == 1.0'])`: the numbers the constraints read are `drag_force` and `lift_force` (the force of the flow on the body along the flow and across it) and `volume_fraction` / `volume` (of the body's own), and a ratio is a formula (`maximize('lift_force / drag_force')`). The design is one density per element on the optimiser of the structural analyses (the filter, the sharpening, the symmetry, `keep` / `avoid` / `extrude`, the method of moving asymptotes), the sensitivity of each force is the exact adjoint of the flow, one adjoint solve for each force the constraints read (they share the factorisation). An element's friction follows its density linearly (`alpha_max rho`; Borrvall & Petersson's convex interpolation was tried: the optimiser parked its volume as a cloud of grey that costs no drag, and every sharpening step took the cloud away at once), the sharpening goes up a little at every iteration over the first three quarters of the run and waits while a condition is broken, and the body is cut from the density at the level that keeps the design's own volume. It replaces `flow_topology_optimization` (a level set that the boundary was moved on by a projected gradient, with a volume bound and a drag and lift objective only), which is gone. A flow wants about 80 iterations (the default for a flow). The result counts the pieces the body is in (`.pieces`) and says so when part of the volume it was asked to keep sits in small pieces of its own: the optimiser's friction lets the flow through a piece of an element or two, so it charges them little, and the real flow around them costs more. **How far the numbers can be trusted is the mesh's:** the real drag of one lens of the post's area, moved along the flow in steps of 0.2 mm on elements of 2 mm, ranges over 15 % (4.06e-7 to 4.75e-7 N), over 5.5 % on elements of 1.5 mm, so a difference between two bodies that is smaller than that is not a difference of the bodies (`docs/analysis.md`).
- **The flow of a problem is the same flow every time, on every machine.** Three things in the flow solver depended on something other than the problem: the incomplete LU that preconditions the linear solves was kept or built again by a rule that compared two clock readings (a busy machine gave the same flow another preconditioner, and so other bits: two runs of the optimiser on the same input differed from the second iteration on, and a design that differs by one part in 10^8 can be cut into a different number of pieces), the number of boxes it is split into was the number of cores of the machine, and the parallel sums (the dot products of the structural solvers, the legacy solvers') were cut into as many parts as there are cores. The rule counts iterations now, the number of boxes follows the size of the problem, the sums have a fixed partition (`TetFlowProblem::solve`, `FlowIlut::build`, `parallelSum`).
- **The mesh of a symmetric problem is symmetric.** A flow past a post or a lens that is exactly symmetric about the plane through the middle of its slab had a force across the flow of 3 % of the drag (5 % through the slab on 1.5 mm elements) and a body that the optimiser reported as kept symmetric came out 20 % asymmetric in area: the lattice the mesh is cut from is not symmetric about the plane unless the plane is a lattice plane (at 1.5 mm it is not), and the cutting of the lattice by the shape is not equivariant under a mirror however the lattice is placed (a quadrilateral is cut along the diagonal through its lowest-numbered vertex, a vertex with two equal crossings goes to the first -- numbers a mirror reverses). `findMeshMirrors` finds the planes through the middle of the region that the shape is symmetric about, `meshShapeMirrored` meshes the part of the shape on one side of them, with the planes as boundaries, and mirrors it (vertices on a plane are shared, the others get an image, the mirrored tetrahedra swap two vertices), and drops a plane whose mirrored boundary does not lie on the shape; the flow's `prepare` uses it, a mesh of an eighth of the slab costs less to make as well. **The half mesh has a corner where the plane meets the surface, and its vertices are moved onto the surface.** The first version dropped many of its planes (about z, and on 1.5 mm elements for the flow around a body: boundary faces standing once, away from the surface): the half mesh is cut at the plane, the surface meets the plane in a crease that the lattice rounds, and a vertex on the plane up to four fifths of an element from the surface belonged to the faces of the surface -- a notch in the mirrored whole that the mirror image cannot continue. The vertices on a plane that belong to a face of the surface are moved along the plane onto the surface now, each to the nearest crossing in the plane, found by the SIDE of the field (16 directions in the plane, stepped out to an element and halved: no gradient is read, so a field made of parts or a mesh distance works as well as an exact one), and the check of the whole allows a fiftieth of an element from the surface, because the two sides of an imported part are tessellated on their own (0.003 of an element was measured on the hole rings of the bracket). A plane that still fails is dropped (the largest set that holds is used) and the plain lattice is used for it -- `FIELDES_TET_DEBUG=1` says which planes held and, for a plane that did not, the first vertices off the shape. **Measured:** the bracket of the examples is meshed mirrored about its middle plane (its first mode, 1507 Hz, moves both lugs alike; on the plain lattice one lug moved twice as far as the other); a beam of 60 x 20 x 6 mm about all three planes, with the finite-difference check of its compliance agreeing to 2e-4 or better; a post or a lens in the slab of example 17 about both its planes (the optimiser's mesh about all three), and the lift force of the one flow around the final body that example draws (`.flow`) is 1e-11 of its drag (it was 1.5 %). Flow results of earlier versions are not read back (the cache key says `mesh=2`). The structural and the thermal analyses are meshed the same way now: a design that is kept symmetric on a mesh whose mirror pairs of elements differ has a gradient that is not the design's own -- the finite-difference check of a thermal design had the derivative of the volume, a linear number, 17 % off, and five digits right on a mirrored mesh.
- **A design that is kept symmetric stays symmetric.** The body the optimiser made came out 14-20 % asymmetric in area (its level field differed from its mirror image by up to 2 mm, from the fifth iteration on) although the output said it was kept symmetric. Three causes in `runDensityDesign`: the partner of an element was the element that holds the mirror image of its centre, which is not a pairing on a mesh that is not exactly symmetric (two elements may name the same one, a pair may not name each other) and a design averaged over such a map stays lopsided; the lines of an extrusion (`extrude='z'`) were bins counted from the lowest centre, not symmetric about the plane, so that elements that mirror each other fell into lines of another make-up; and the design was never put back on the symmetric side after a step, so a rounding difference grew (a symmetric design is often a saddle of the asymmetric ones). The partners are exact pairs now (elements that name each other, then the nearest of the rest, each in one pair), the lines are counted from the plane outward on both sides, the sensitivities are symmetrised after the sum along the lines too, and the design is symmetrised after every step. The mirrored mesh chooses its planes one by one (the largest set of planes for which the mirror holds) instead of dropping the last one when the check fails, which dropped the good plane of a slab whose other plane did not hold.
- **A design that is kept symmetric about several planes is averaged over the orbits of its elements.** The sensitivities, the start and the design after every step were averaged with the partner of each element plane after plane; that is a projection only when the pairs of the planes agree, and on a mesh that is not exactly symmetric they need not. The elements joined by the pairs of any plane are one orbit now and the average over an orbit is the one operation: a projection whatever the pairs are (applied twice it changes nothing, and it is its own transpose, so the sensitivity of a symmetric design is the average of the sensitivities); an orbit larger than the images an element can have (two for each plane) is left alone (`runDensityDesign`, `density_design.cpp`).
- **The real drag of a body in a flow is the mean over several places of the mesh lattice, with its spread.** A mesh of tetrahedra on a lattice meets a body at some place, and the force on the body depends on it: one lens of the post's area, moved along the flow in steps, ranged over 14 % (2 mm elements) and 5.5 % (1.5 mm), the round post over 26 %, and the lattice's phase in y and z moves it by 8 % more -- so one mesh is not an answer. The flow around the final body is solved on the lattice at 8 places (4 for a flow of more than 100 000 elements, 2 above 300 000, one above a million; a low-discrepancy sequence over one period of the lattice), `.real_drag` and `.real_lift` are the means, `.real_drag_sd` the standard deviation of one place, `.real_drags` the forces, the verified numbers and `best.drag_force` the means; the result says how many places, the spread and the error of the mean, and says when a body is too thin for its mesh (a spread of more than 5 %). With it the lens test of the optimiser's body (a lens is the body of least drag, a body below the best lens means the solver is wrong) reads: -6 % on 2 mm elements (1.4 sigma: nothing can be told), +2 % on 1.5 mm and +1 % on 1.0 mm (not below the lens).
- **An iteration of the flow optimiser is cheaper.** A flow solved again after a small change of the design starts from the last flow: it goes straight to Newton's method, without the Stokes start that used to throw the last flow away (a first residual above a fifth takes the Picard way, as before), and it is solved to 1e-5 of the load (the drag of a flow differs by 1.3e-6 between 1e-5 and 1e-9). The adjoint is solved to a reduction of 1e-6 on an incomplete LU of its own, so that the factorisation of the flow is no longer replaced, and built again, at every iteration, and a factorisation made for a Picard matrix is not kept for a Newton one. The flow around the final body is still solved to 1e-9, and the finite-difference check of the sensitivities (`FIELDES_TOPO_FDCHECK`) now evaluates the flow tightly, as the one of the structural designs does. Which solves are taken is decided by counts and never by a clock, so the result is the same on every run.
- **A heat conduction is designed by the same call, on tetrahedra.** `topology_optimization(thermal_analysis(...), constraints=[minimize('max_temperature'), 'volume_fraction <= 0.3'])` replaces `thermal_topology_optimization` (the voxel optimiser with its one goal, the heat-weighted mean temperature of the heat inputs, and its one limit, the volume, is gone). The problem is the thermal analysis it is given -- its part, held temperatures, heat inputs, heat generations, convection and material --, the design is one density to each tetrahedron on the optimiser every analysis shares (the filter of the design, the sharpening, symmetry, `extrude`, `keep` and `avoid`, the steps taken back, the live picture, the cache), and any number of the temperature is a goal or a limit: `max_temperature` and `min_temperature` (p-norms of the nodes' temperature), `max_heat_flux`, `input_temperature` (the temperature the heat comes in at: the old goal), `temperature(x, y, z)`, with `volume_fraction`, `volume` and `mass`, each with its sensitivity from one adjoint solve. The elements that hold a temperature or carry a heat input stay solid; a heat generation is a condition of the design space, as in the classic volume-to-point problem (its heat is made where its region is, material or not: heat made all through a part and one small sink grow branching conductors); material that nothing joins to a held temperature or a convecting surface is taken out. The thermal analysis has `input_temperature`, `volume` and `mass` now. Results kept by an earlier version are solved again (the cache's version is 5).
